## Methodology
| Past and projected mortality rates (qₓ) from the 2024-based Great Britain life tables |
| --- |
| Life tables |
| The life table describes the course of mortality throughout the life cycle. It is a method of analysing age-specific death rates and is a standard demographic tool used to measure expectation of life by age. For more information, see: |
| National life tables Quality and methodology information (QMI) report |
| Period and cohort life tables are produced biennially based on assumptions for future mortality from the National population projections (NPPs). These tables give historic and projected life expectancies by single year of age and sex. The life tables also provide us with other mortality data including the probability of dying between exact age x and x+1 (qₓ), and the numbers surviving at exact age x (lₓ). |
| Period life tables are calculated using age-specific death rates for a given period with no allowance for any actual or projected future changes in mortality. In contrast, cohort life tables are calculated using age-specific death rates which allow for known or projected changes in mortality throughout a person’s life. For more information on the differences between period and cohort life tables, see: |
| ‘Guide to period and cohort life expectancy’ |
| qₓ |
| qₓ is the probability of dying between exact age x and (x+1), and is displayed in the following tables per 100,000 people, therefore creating a mortality rate. All the other functions in a life table can be calculated from this, for example eₓ (life expectancy). |
| The qₓ mortality rates in these spreadsheets are for calendar years. For 1981 to 2024, they are calculated from the calendar year birth registrations and death registrations data and the mid-year population estimates. For 2025 to 2124, they are derived from the National population projections mid-year qₓ mortality rates. |
| Data within these spreadsheets are provided by single year of age from 0 to 125 years inclusive. Mortality rates at the oldest ages (over 100 years) are highly uncertain and should be treated with caution; they have been published as a user requested dataset for research/modelling purposes. |
| Interpreting the tables |
| Period tables |
| In the period qₓ tables for both males and females, ‘year’ corresponds to the year of interest. For example, if you were interested in the year 2024, you would navigate to 2024 across the top of the table. ‘Exact age (years)’ is the age of interest, for example, the 99th birthday. If you navigate down the table to age 99 years and across to the corresponding year, 2024, a qₓ rate is displayed. This is the probability (per 100,000) of someone aged exactly 99 years in the year 2024 dying before reaching age 100. |
| Cohort tables |
| In the cohort qₓ tables for both males and females, ‘year of birth’ corresponds to the year that the cohort was born, for example, the group of people that were born in 1987. ‘Exact age (years)’ is the age of interest, for example, the 99th birthday. If you were interested in the year 1987 and age 99 years, you would navigate down the table to age 99 years and across to the year 1987. This means that you are looking at someone aged 99 years in 2086, because they were born in 1987. The qₓ rate that is displayed is the projected probability (per 100,000) of someone aged 99 years who was born in 1987 dying before reaching age 100 years. |
| Note: When using the cohort probabilities of dying, the user should be aware that any calculation may include assumed future mortality improvements and that these become less reliable the further into the future from the projections base year. |
| For further guidance and examples of how to use these datasets, please see: |
| Guide to interpreting past and projected period and cohort life tables |
